NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I tested the new AI-powered tool on ingredient swaps, recipe scaling, and a missing Bundt pan.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Around 7PM each weeknight, I find myself standing in front of the fridge, trying to decide on something for dinner that won’t make my husband complain, “Chicken again?” Yes, I should meal plan more, but I don’t, so this is where I’m at. A nightly battle in which the chicken usually wins.

Recently, my best weapon in this fight has been AI. Between Alexa Plus in the Echo smart display on my kitchen counter, the Samsung Food app on my fridge, and even an AI-powered cooking robot, AI has become my go-to sous chef — albeit one I need to keep a close eye on. It’s helped me swap ingredients, generate ideas, fix things when I mess up, and yes, even cook better. So I was intrigued when my go-to recipe app, NYT Cooking, introduced an AI assistant.

Another great feature of the service, which I’ve been paying for for several years, is the reader comments. Here, I’ve often found solutions to cooking dilemmas, like what to substitute for makrut lime leaves — there’s always one slightly bougie ingredient in an NYT Cooking recipe that I don’t have on hand.

However, reading through hundreds of comments to find answers at 7PM on a weeknight isn’t always practical. And this is where I’ve found the NYT Cooking assistant helpful. Among other things, it was trained on user comments, and in my few days testing it, it provided practical substitutions and solid cooking guidance for the new ingredients. It also won’t unquestionably agree to crazy requests (I tried!). As with all AI tools, it’s not perfect — it almost made me put half the required butter in my cake — but as a tool, it is useful and feels like a solid application of AI. Importantly, it doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you.

It doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you

The assistant appears in the app in a small text box on the recipe page with a prompt: “Customize this recipe…” From here, you type in a question to adapt it to your needs. This can be as simple as “What can I substitute for this ingredient?” to “Scale this recipe to feed seven people, and make it vegan and gluten-free.” The New York Times has also said the feature can suggest substitutions for cooking equipment and convert measurements; the latter option has been in the app for a couple of years.

Last Friday night, instead of staring aimlessly into the fridge, I turned to the Chicken folder in my NYT Cooking app Recipe Box and found a One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger I’d been wanting to try. Only I didn’t have chicken thighs or half the ingredients.

I could have figured these steps myself, but it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible.

I typed my dilemma into the assistant box on the recipe page: “I have chicken tenders, fresh ginger, garlic, rice, broth, limes, but no chard or cilantro or scallions. I do have green beans and red onions.” Within seconds, it created a new version of the recipe, with a new title: One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger and Green Beans. It suggested a shorter browning time for the tenders, cooking the red onion alongside the chicken, and steaming the green beans on top of the rice for the last eight minutes instead of the chard. Every change was highlighted in the new recipe under a Your Version tab, and I could flip back to the original at any point.

I noticed it hadn’t adjusted for the lack of cilantro, saying it was optional. So I asked if I could use Thai basil, which I happen to have, and it agreed, highlighting how it would change the flavor. While I could have figured out a lot of these steps myself, maybe with the help of Google, it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible and nice not to have to swipe out of the app. The NYT Cooking editors say it’s been trained not to comply with requests that will taste bad.

The resulting dish — which took the same amount of time to cook as the original — was delicious and got my husband’s seal of approval (he just had the leftovers for lunch). It was very different from the recipe Yasmin Fahr wrote, and I can see the purists’ point. But the rewritten recipe was much easier to use and produced a better dish than winging it with my own subs gleaned from the comments section, which I usually do with varying degrees of success.

I cook regularly, so I had a sense of what ingredients I had on hand that might work for the dish I wanted to make. But the assistant can also help if you have no idea what to use as a substitute. For example, I was cooking a Thai red curry and asked what I could use instead of makrut lime leaf. It replied, saying that grated lime zest would work, but “it misses the deeper floral, herbal aroma” of fresh limes. (See? Bougie.)

Over the weekend, I tried several more swaps, asking whether half-and-half would work instead of cream in a caramel apple Dutch baby, or if Castelvetrano olives and smoked paprika could stand in for kalamata and chile pepper in a roasted halibut dish. Swapping spices is always a bold move in any dish, as it can really ruin the flavor.

I also had the app convert the Dutch baby recipe’s metric weights to US measurements, which it did seamlessly. Then I threw in some curveballs. How about using fresh cayenne peppers with the halibut? It warned me that substituting cayenne for chile “brings significant, sharp heat.” Could I swap ladyfingers for minced beef in this trifle (à la Rachel Green in Friends)? “Minced beef cannot be used in place of the ladyfingers or anywhere in this sweet dessert trifle.” Good call.

While I was cooking, I compared the NYT Cooking assistant with Alexa Plus, Amazon’s generative-AI voice assistant I regularly use to help me cook via the Echo Show 11 smart display in my kitchen. Interestingly, it offered the same guidance as the Cooking assistant on substitutions for the chicken and rice dish, but with slightly different cooking times. Alexa pulls some recipes from the NYT app through a partnership between the companies.

However, Alexa couldn’t show the modified recipe on its screen; it could only tell me the changes while showing the original. When I asked it to walk me through them, it got confused and improvised, telling me to cut the chicken into bite-sized pieces — which wasn’t in the modified recipe. Overall, I found using the Cooking app more reliable, although messier. It would be great to be able to use voice control to navigate the app, as I can with Alexa on my smart display, rather than using my garlicky fingers.

It modified the recipe’s cooking time from three hours to 50 minutes and split the batter across two 12-muffin tins. But that was going to make 24 muffins, way too many for me. So I asked it to halve the recipe to get 12 muffins. This led to some smart choices, like halving five eggs to three instead of an unhelpful two and a half.

For science, I bought a Bundt pan to make the original version too. Both turned out great, but things got a bit sticky when I swapped between the original recipe and the AI version in the app. You can’t save your new recipe separately, which caused confusion when I was cooking the original. Instead of scrolling back through the recipe with sticky fingers, I asked the assistant how much butter I needed (using voice typing), and it gave me the modified recipe’s 6 tablespoons rather than the original’s three-quarter cup, without telling me which version it was using. You can delete your version, which I would advise doing to avoid issues like this — 6 tablespoons of butter would have made for a dry cake.

If you already read all the comments before starting a New York Times recipe, then you will probably find this tool offers a similar experience in a more streamlined way. For everyday problems — wrong cut of chicken, no cilantro, or no Bundt cake pan — it was genuinely helpful and saved me a trip to the store. It was also smart enough to know when to say no to minced beef in a trifle.

As with every AI tool I’ve used in the kitchen, the NYT Cooking AI assistant works well as a sous chef, but it’s not the head chef — I still felt the need to double-check amounts and cooking times and cross-reference them with the original to make sure it hadn’t done something outlandish.

If you’re a purist, you can just ignore the assistant. While you can’t turn off the chat box, it’s easy to scroll past. I do think it will make me use the app more often, since I no longer need to worry as much when I don’t have makrut lime leaves on hand or the right cut of meat. For me, standing in front of the fridge with chicken tenders and no plan, it’s been a useful addition to my nightly cooking routine.

European Commission advises TikTok to redesign FYP or risk fines

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

European regulators warn TikTok's "rabbit hole"-inducing FYP might be breaking EU law.

Preliminary findings from an investigation by the European Commission accuse the social media giant of failing to mitigate the potential mental health risks of its personalized algorithmic feed in violation of the Digital Services Act.

In particular, the regulatory body takes issue with the platform's "continuous stream of personalized videos" and the ways in which its algorithm determines user interest. Repeated interaction with triggering content, the report says, is interpreted as positive interest, leading to a "rabbit hole effect."

"This design can be harmful when such endless and repeated exposure involves, for example, content about sadness, isolation or body image," the Commission writes. "These ‘rabbit holes' can have a detrimental impact on users' mental health, especially that of vulnerable adults and minors." The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The early findings urged TikTok redesign its For Your Page. "TikTok should rely more on users' explicit preferences, disable viewing time as an indicator to recommend content, increase the accessibility and usability of user controls, and regularly test its algorithm," advise regulators.

If TikTok fails to comply with the commission's final report and recommendations, the company may be fined up to 6 percent of its global revenue.

Studies of TikTok's content recommendation algorithm have found that the platform's content moderation and personalization options frequently fail to keep harmful content off of users' feeds. The company has previously been accused of knowingly proceeding with ineffective measures to reduce excessive screen time and harmful content, including the tendency of users to fall into "bubbles" of harmful content via their For Your Page.

This past week, TikTok, Meta, and Google each filed lawsuits against California attorney general Rob Banta, seeking to block a new state law that prohibits "addictive" and "psychologically exploitative" design features on social media platforms. Such features include infinite scroll, autoplay, and personalized recommendations.

Fortnite players demand Epic reveal odds for ultra-rare Pirate Shop Token after days of hunting

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Fortnite players are calling on Epic Games to reveal the odds of finding Pirate Shop Access after days of failed chest hunts, conflicting community estimates, and claims that a server-side system called “Clover” controls the reward.

Pirate Shop Access was added with the Treasure Hunters mini-season on November 1. Players who find it receive two activations, each opening a 14-day window during which eligible Item Shop cosmetics can be claimed without spending V-Bucks. Real-money offers, gifting, and Battle Pass rewards are excluded.

Epic has not published a percentage explaining how often Pirate Shop Access can appear. Fortnite has only said that the reward works like a normal Battle Royale chest and that every player has an equal opportunity to find it. That statement does not explain whether the chance is one in 100, one in 10,000, or the rumored 1/700,000.

Hey @Pirat_Nation – Buying an account or using fake tools is a scam. This is just like any of the chests in Battle Royale, so every player has an equal opportunity to find Pirate Shop Access.

The lack of an official figure has allowed several different estimates to spread online, with no way for players to verify which, if any, is accurate. That has become especially frustrating for players who have spent hours opening chests without knowing whether they are experiencing normal bad luck or chasing a reward with an effectively unreachable chance.

Streamer Jynxzi has reportedly spent more than 75 hours searching for the token, while Stable Ronaldo claims he’s spent over 70 hours hunting. Both streamers have called on Epic to publish the odds.

The “Clover” claims have added another layer to the controversy. Fortnite leaker Egyptian_Leaker claimed Epic created a system that can adjust the token’s odds without exposing the numbers in the game files. Llamalaxy later claimed that the reward roll is handled by a live backend service when a chest is opened, which would explain why dataminers cannot find a fixed percentage in the client.

Fortnite’s ultra-rare Pirate Coin hunt sparks paid carries, AFK tools, and a $10,000 eBay listing

Fortnite adds rare reward that makes entire Item Shop free for 14 days, but there’s limits

Epic Games gives update to Fortnite players claiming they still haven’t been paid

Those claims have not been independently confirmed by Epic.

Epic’s official Treasure Hunters announcement says Treasure Hours will feature boosted drops, but it does not specifically confirm whether Pirate Shop Access becomes more likely during those events.

Fortnite made a whole new dev system just to hide the Pirate Shop Token odds 😭– They named this system "Clover"– It ensures that they can modify the odds without the leakers knowing pic.twitter.com/61Il08YCv0

That leaves players with several unanswered questions. Are the odds fixed for the entire season? Are they the same for every player and chest type? Can Epic change them during the event? Do Treasure Hours affect the token at all?

Traditional gambling definitions generally involve chance, a prize, and something of value being risked. A federal definition of a bet or wager specifically excludes contests where participants risk only personal effort or internet access, although state laws differ.

Because Pirate Shop Access is earned by playing Fortnite and opening chests without paying V-Bucks for each attempt, the available facts are not enough to call the system illegal gambling.

The FTC has discussed both paid loot boxes and random rewards earned through gameplay. Its guidance focuses on whether odds or costs are presented accurately and without misleading players. The agency’s 2025 action involving Genshin Impact concerned paid loot boxes, virtual currency, and allegedly misleading odds.

Based on those public materials, the absence of a published Pirate Shop percentage alone does not establish an FTC violation. The key questions would be whether Epic made misleading claims about the reward, whether players were required or encouraged to spend money, and whether the odds changed without accurate disclosure.

Epic’s public response has addressed equal access and scams, but it has not provided the numerical odds players are asking for.

Blackburn Rovers 0 – 0 West Ham United | Half time report

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

However it was Blackburn who almost struck first thanks to an overlap down their right and a shot that looped agonisingly over Hermansens’ goal. Mayers responded by fizzing one cross just out of reach of Piroe -nil nil at 20 minutes.

Engels, Austbo and Mayers combined well as the Hammers attacked repeatedly down Blackburn’s left, Austbø impressing in place of Solomon. Half an hour gone and few scares for West Ham:  A more competitive, aggressive start than in recent weeks. Piroe had one ball played through but his lack of pace allowed defenders to crowd him out.

The half continued in the same vein, West Ham looking well capable of scoring with most of the attacking coming down the flanks, Mayers on the overlap giving Scott Parker a new dimension to his Hammers offensive work.

Worryingly, Engels went over on his ankle just on the stroke of half time –  the teams went in at 0 – 0, a far more measured, controlled half than West Ham fans have been treated to in recent weeks.

The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies.

The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Concerns are being raised that neither the Commerce Department nor Nvidia are doing their due diligence when it comes to vetting Chinese firms cleared to handle the company’s H200 AI chips. According to The New York Times, New Matrix is on the list of approved firms, yet its offices are empty, “a hollow gray shell with wires dangling from the ceiling where lights should be.” In fact, the paper could find little evidence that the company exists in any meaningful way:

Only scant information can be found online about New Matrix, which was created 17 months ago. At the building that New Matrix lists as its address, a company directory does not include the firm. At the nearby property management agency for the industrial park, a receptionist and a manager both said they had no record of the company.

What little information can be found about New Matrix is concerning. Its owners have ties to a Chinese private equity firm that has been blacklisted by the US government since 2024. And according to the Times, this is not a rare lapse in oversight, but a troubling pattern of companies connected to entities with national-security-related trade restrictions being approved to purchase Nvidia chips.

Nvidia had been restricted from selling chips to China during the Biden presidency. But the Trump administration relaxed those limits after Nvidia agreed to give the government a 15-percent cut of all sales. The company’s CEO, Jensen Huang, also launched an intense lobbying campaign and was apparently targeted by China’s Ministry of State Security in a spying plot.

American, Delta, And United Accused Of Collusion To Keep Airfares High

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, United Airlines, and other major carriers face new lawsuits alleging they share sensitive pricing and shopping data to keep airfares higher than they would otherwise be. While watching competitors’ prices is ordinary business, pooling information in a way that discourages competition deserves a much closer look.

View From The Wing highlights two proposed class actions targeting the Airline Tariff Publishing Company (ATPCO), its subsidiary 3Victors, and major U.S. airlines.

The Washington complaint names airline companies including American, Delta, United, Southwest, Alaska, Hawaiian, and JetBlue. A separate Virginia complaint targets ATPCO, 3Victors, and a smaller group of carriers.

A central question these lawsuits raise is worth examining: does greater visibility into competitors’ pricing help airlines offer better deals, or make them less inclined to offer deals at all?

ATPCO is an airline-owned company that has long distributed fares and fare rules. The complaints challenge an expansion into pooled shopping data, alleging that participating airlines exchange commercially sensitive information through a common platform and use it to coordinate pricing.

Shopping data can reveal demand before a passenger purchases anything. Searching for a flight generates information about the itinerary requested and the offers returned, even if the customer closes the browser without booking.

On its own website, 3Victors describes participation in Iris this way:

“The more you contribute, the more you receive.”

Its PriceEye description promotes alerts and tools that identify market undercuts and overpricing. Meanwhile, DemandView promises insight into where travelers want to go before they book.

Those descriptions explain why airlines would find the products useful but they do not, at least by descriptions alone, establish that using them violates antitrust law.

The plaintiffs’ concern is that exchanging this information reduces the uncertainty that encourages airlines to compete.

Consider this example. An airline has seats to fill and thinks a lower fare could attract passengers from a competitor. Cutting the price makes sense if it can win enough additional business.

But suppose its competitors can spot and match the discount almost immediately. The airline may gain little additional business while collecting less money from passengers who would have booked anyway. That could make discounting less attractive in the first place.

But there’s a difference between that (legitimate) concern and proving that these particular arrangements unlawfully suppress competition…we’d need to see evidence about what the airlines actually exchange and how they use it.

A sophisticated pricing dashboard is not necessarily evidence of a conspiracy, for I can think of a counterexample.

If American discovers that United is charging $100 less for a comparable flight, it might lower its fare. Passengers benefit. Better information about demand could also help an airline identify an opportunity to add service.

I use comparison tools when buying tickets…I would hardly expect an airline to set its prices without looking at what competitors charge.

The harder question concerns reciprocal access to sensitive information that competitors would not otherwise possess. What exactly is being contributed? How current and detailed is it? Does the arrangement support independent decisions or weaken the incentive to compete?

My concern as a passenger is straightforward. Airlines have become very good at identifying how much we might be willing to pay. I want them to remain equally concerned that another carrier will offer us a better deal. An industry in which competitors can readily anticipate one another’s behavior deserves scrutiny, particularly when the information comes from an exchange they collectively support.

If these cases proceed into discovery, I will be interested in what the contracts and internal communications reveal…I am not ready to declare these lawsuits winners or losers, though I think View From The Wing is probably right that this airlines appear to have th stronger arguments.

Two new lawsuits allege that major U.S. airlines are using shared shopping and pricing information to weaken competition and sustain higher fares. The companies’ public product descriptions establish that extensive competitive data tools exist, though that does not prove unlawful conduct.

Better information can produce lower fares. It can also make an airline more confident that it need not offer one. I want airlines competing aggressively for my business, and these cases raise a fair question about whether their shared data arrangements encourage that competition or undermine it, a fascinating lawsuit indeed.

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

Looking for a handout, after Tuesday look for more of this type stuff. The lazy will be looking for payback on corporate America and crazy judges just might give it to them. It’s going to be a bad time to work hard and make tough life choices that make you successful in America. You are going to have a target on your back, hopefully not in the Luigi way.

This platform shouldn’t exist at all. For work I submit quotes and proposals including pricing in a very competitive highly regulated market. The idea that this is a legitimate tool outside of enabling collusion is absurd.

Matthew, framing this as mere revenue management misses what’s actually happening here. I’m hoping these suits get somewhere, though I’m not holding my breath given how high the legal bar is for proving actual price-fixing. But let’s be real… when airlines pool real-time search data through a middleman, it gives them all the pricing discipline of a cartel without having to sit in a room together. Just because it might clear current legal loopholes doesn’t mean it isn’t screwing over us passengers.

Welcome to 20th century American “capialism.” The return of oligarchy. Tome for a new American Revolution that puts consumers forst.

It is past-TIME to rebuild sensible guardrails to protect consumers FIRST. (A few light edits…)

Elvis Presley Rights Owners Sue Suno Over AI Voice Cloning: ‘Elvis’ Identity Was Stolen’

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The singer's legendary voice is at the center of a cutting-edge lawsuit that aims to hold the AI firm legally liable for allowing users to create knockoff vocals.

The owners of Elvis Presley’s intellectual property are suing Suno in a new lawsuit that claims the artificial intelligence music company violated the King of Rock ‘n’ Roll’s likeness rights by stealing his voice.

Weeks after suing AI giant Anthropic for training Claude on Elvis songs, the new case filed Friday (Nov. 6) by Elvis Presley Enterprises levels similar copyright accusations against Suno, the leading AI music firm. But it also includes legally-novel allegations that Suno broke the law by letting users clone Presley’s “unmistakable and iconic voice.”

“All of the humanity, history, despair, and triumph that constitutes Elvis’ identity was stolen and is stored on servers and harnessed by Suno to make massive amounts of money,” writes Richard Busch, a veteran music litigator at the firm Adams & Reese who filed the case. “Suno did so intentionally, maliciously, and fraudulently to financially benefit itself and artificially compete with Elvis’ likeness and copyrights, and continues to do so to this day.”

The new lawsuit marks one of the first major cases under a new Tennessee law, coincidentally called the ELVIS Act, that was recently enacted to address problems caused by AI. The first-in-the-nation statute was touted as a weapon against people who create illegal deepfakes, but it also allows for lawsuits against tech firms that enable such voice and image cloning in the first place.

Taken together with the Anthropic case, the suits over Elvis represent easily most significant action filed on behalf of an individual musician over AI — in this case, arguably the most famous recording artist of all time. Suno and Anthropic were already facing cases from music labels accusing them of infringing millions of copyrighted songs to train their models, as well as a class action by smaller artists who similarly say Suno stole their likenesses.

Friday’s lawsuit was filed by Elvis Presley Enterprises, a unit of the licensing giant Authentic Brands Group that controls the intellectual property tied to Elvis, including much of his song catalog and the rights to his name, image and likeness. In a statement to Billboard on Saturday, ABG spokeswoman Lark-Marie Antón said the new lawsuit and its evidence “speak for themselves.”

“The name, image, likeness, and music of Elvis Presley are among the most valuable intellectual property assets in the world,” Antón said. “We protect those rights vigorously.”

In statement Saturday responding to the lawsuit, a Suno spokeswoman told Billboard: “Suno exists to help people create new, original music, not to trade on anyone’s name or likeness, and we prohibit any unauthorized use of an artist’s work. To protect against this, we do not allow prompts for specific artists’ names and songs and work with third party providers to screen uploaded audio files and lyrics for potential misuse. We are always working to strengthen our guardrails and we will continue to add new safeguards that protect artists and our users.”

The looming battle with Suno will raise untested legal questions about the extent to which AI companies can be sued for allegedly ripping off a musical artist’s identity. The use of AI to convincingly mimic real people has been one of the booming technology’s most controversial applications, and no where less so than an industry largely built on famous singing voices.

“Elvis’ voice possessed a distinctively rich vocal timbre, with a warm velvety resonance and perfectly employed vibrato, coupled with his ability to vary his technique and delivery within the same song as well as across several different styles of music, which became his immediately recognizable musical signature across the world,” the lawsuit says.

Suno has argued in other lawsuits that its current models do not allow users to include the names of specific artists in prompts, nor produce outputs that mimic specific real voices. But in Friday’s complaint, the Elvis owners say those claims are “unequivocally false.” They included links to videos capturing the use of Suno’s platform to play songs that sound remarkably similar to Presley.

“Those … are NOT examples of Elvis Presley singing. They are synthetic and unlicensed uses of Elvis’ voice and likeness,” Busch writes. “There are hundreds to thousands more like it.”

The new case will test the power and limits of the ELVIS (Ensuring Likeness Voice and Image Security) Act, a law enacted in 2024 in Presley’s adopted home state to make it easier for musicians to bring such cases. Existing laws typically only applied to commercial exploitation of someone’s likeness, like an unauthorized advertisement; the new statute removed those restrictions. ELVIS also specifically imposed liability on companies that provide tools allowing for unauthorized use of a person’s likeness.

“Suno is the embodiment of why the ELVIS Act was so essentially required,” the lawsuit reads. “Suno has stolen Elvis’ voice, his image, and likeness, as well as his copyrighted works on, literally, a breathtaking scale, and must now be called to answer for its theft.”

Suno’s lawyers have argued such claims won’t hold up in court. In an earlier likeness case filed by Jason Isbell and other artists, the AI firm’s lawyers argued it merely uses artist names as “shorthand for the genre they perform in” and does not allow for direct theft of their voices. But the new lawsuit says that as recently as last month, Suno generated an Elvis clone based simply on lyrics that included his name.

“Entering a musician’s name into Suno’s ‘Create’ function, such as Elvis’, causes Suno to generate unauthorized output based on his [name, image and likeness], which copies and reproduces the signature and recognizable aspects of his distinguished voice,” Busch writes in the new case.

In addition to the new voice claims, the lawsuit echoes the same accusations made against Anthropic over training on copyrighted Elvis songs, including “Can’t Help Falling in Love,” arguably Presley’s most famous track; “Don’t Be Cruel,” one of his early massive hits; and “A Little Less Conversation,” “All Shook Up,” “Return to Sender” and “(You’re the) Devil in Disguise”. And like the earlier case, the new lawsuit claims Suno stole those songs in order to replace human artists.

“The next Elvis won’t pick up a guitar,” the Presley Enterprises lawyers write. “Suno’s ravenous race to the bottom aims to make human creativity obsolete, coldly replicating without a license in seconds what takes years to learn, struggle with, maintain, and endlessly attempt to make better and perfect.”

Separately on Friday, the same attorneys another case against Suno on behalf of Eight Mile Style, a publisher that owns much of Eminem’s catalog. That case includes similar copyright allegations as the Elvis case, accusing the lab of training its models on the rapper’s “Lose Yourself,” “Without Me,” “Till I Collapse,” “The Real Slim Shady, “Stan” and other hits.

The Elvis and Eminem cases add to mounting legal challenges for Suno. The company was first sued in 2024 by all three major music companies over AI training. Though it settled with Warner Music Group last year and has vowed to partner with the music industry, is still litigating with UMG and Sony Music — who have shown no interest in striking a deal. It has also faced new cases in recent months from music publishers Round Hill Music and Reservoir, as well as the Isbell case over likenesses.

Skyrim's lead designer hopes The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel finally fixes the series' ladder problem

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Bruce Nesmith also hopes for a "better implementation of mounts"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Skyrim's lead designer has shared what he hopes to see in The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel - top of the list: better mounts and, erm, ladders!

Bruce Nesmith was one of the lead developers on arguably Bethesda Game Studios' most enduring hit, the high fantasy open world of Skyrim, but his hopes and dreams for Sentinel are a little more modest than you might expect. While some fans are running wild with lore theories and hopes for ambitious new mechanics like sailing, Nesmith just wants to be able to climb ladders.

Speaking to The Examined Game (below), Nesmith says the request is "a very strange one," but not being able to climb ladders "really limits the art team in terms of how you can do multi-layer [environments]." There's a very good reason why Skyrim didn't let you properly climb up and down ladders, however.

"All the ladders in Skyrim, you click on the ladder, poof, you're at the top or you have to climb a stair. But to do ladders, you have to deal with a whole host of questions which our programmers very legitimately put in front of us and said, 'You give me answers to these, I'll tell you how much programming time it's going to take to make them.' And you know, that caused problems right there," Nesmith explained.

He then points to what would happen if an NPC is halfway down a ladder as you decide to climb up, and all the development questions that pop up from that seemingly small scenario: "Does the player go through the NPC? Is he blocked by the NPC? Does the NPC stay on the ladder? Does the NPC go back up and know to wait and then come back down?[...] And I'm just scratching the surface of some of the problems they brought up. So, it seems simple. and other games have climbing ladders, but we chose to put our efforts into other things to make them better."

The Bethesda veteran says the small change could really "make the world feel a little more immersive" and affect how the team can design environments. His other small request is a "better implementation of mounts," which he acknowledges is a "common complaint" among Skyrim fans.

"I'm not alone in that. But it turns out mounts are tricky. They're really tricky. There's a lot of hard decisions to be made in those. You can ride a horse, but that's all you can do in Skyrim," he says. "I should be able to do some other things with the horse, too, like maybe fight on the horse or have it fight with me."

1992 Schuppan Porsche 962R: One Man’s Plan to Put a Group C Race Car on the Street

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

From the July 1991 issue of Car and Driver.

If you’ve got a desperate desire to own a modern, 600-horsepower, Porsche-­based street-legal race car and have $1,620,000 to back up this peculiar day­dream, the line forms to the right for one of Vern Schuppan’s Porsche 962CRs.

The good news is it will be a very short line in the United States, with room for only seventeen. (If you’ve got $1,620,000, there isn’t any bad news.)

Schuppan, whose resume includes a victory at Le Mans in 1983, a bronze medal at the ’81 Indianapolis 500, and experience on the Formula 1 tour, plans to build 50 of these Team Schuppan 962s. That’s 962, as in Porsche Official Race Car, twice the winner at Le Mans and three times an IMSA season champion.

With all sorts of real and imagined race-cars-for-the-road getting serious ink these days, Schuppan has been doing in­stead of promising. The 47-year-old Aus­tralian has this Le Mans long-tailed 962 li­censed and fully certified for the road and, as this is written, is close to complet­ing the first of what is to be a limited se­ries of Team Schuppan 962CRs (the no­menclature is “C” for Group C and “R” for road) on the same basis but with unique “GT” bodywork and all the com­fort features one might expect for more than 1.5 million dollars.

See all results for used cars for sale near 14094

Because this project has been commissioned by a Japanese outfit—Art Sport Division, a company that specializes in exotic machinery and is headed by Toshio Terada—most of them will be measured in yen, 220 million or there­abouts. The company says it will sell the first 50 worldwide, with seventeen head­ed for America. The red car shown on these pages is also destined for Japan, for the garage of Hiroyuki Miyake, a real-estate executive and Schuppan racing sponsor who want­ed a Le Mans racer with the minimum of modifications needed to make it street-­legal. A genuine Stuttgart-built 962 (number 962/123), it started life in IMSA with the Primus team. Schuppan rebuilt it as a Group C car with a British-made aluminum honeycomb chassis and took it to Le Mans in 1988, where it was driven by Brian Redman, Jean-Pierre Jarier, and Eje Elgh. It ran sixth for a while and fin­ished tenth. After competing in the Fuji 1000-kilometer race that year, the car was retired. When Schuppan began pon­dering the notion of a roadgoing 962 some twenty months ago, it was there at the back of the shop, a suitable case for treatment.

This is a real-life racer, currently designated as a 962R, and is the prototype for Team Schuppan’s road rockets, the first of which should roll out the doors in Octo­ber. The work done on this car will be ap­plied directly to the “CR” street car, which, unlike this “R” model, will have its nose and headlight area smoothed and finished. (Miyake’s enthusiasm has gen­erated two more orders for replicas of this Le Mans car, so Schuppan now has plans to build five of them in addition to the 50 street cars. They will sell for the same price as the more comfortably equipped GT version.)

See all results for used cars for sale near 14094

All of the “production” models will have Schuppan’s own carbon-fiber monocoque chassis, developed for his 962 race cars, and carbon-fiber and Kevlar bodywork instead of the original Porsche panels, which are mostly fiberglass.

Driving a full-spec Group C car on normal roads should not be a problem—­if the pavement is smooth. But bringing one into compliance with all of the appli­cable regulations is something else. Rather more, in fact, than Vern Schup­pan and his friend and associate Howden Ganley (another ex-racer from Down Under and the man behind the Tiga race-car company) had thought when they embarked on the venture.

The major task involves the engine. This 962 has a Porsche-built, 24-valve, twin-turbo 2.6-liter engine with water­-cooled cylinder heads (later Le Mans cars have displacement of up to 3.0 liters). For the road, it needs to be cleaned, cooled, and quieted down.

A full three-way catalyst system is re­quired in the U.S. and Japan, and if you look down the air-exit ducts on this 962’s tail, two catalytic converters are visible, one on each side. The Bosch Motronic MP 1.2 engine-management system pro­vides the electronic controls needed. Meeting current emissions standards was achieved with relatively little difficulty, while retaining the 962R’s maximum power of 680 horsepower.

The side sponsons of the Group C chassis already have to accommodate three kinds of heat exchange—water and oil radiators as well as turbo intercoolers. And to guard against overheating in traf­fic, Schuppan had to find room for two big electric fans beneath them, to pro­vide airflow at low speeds. Because of these, and also because the tight cockpit has no opening windows, air condition­ing became a must. Schuppan’s team was able to tuck the works of a Sanden air conditioner into the chassis below the passenger door.

By the standards of some race cars, these turbocharged Group C machines are not unduly noisy. When it first ran on the road, this one had what looked to be an undersized single muffler across the back of the car that was fed by two tail­pipes. Since these photographs were tak­en, two more mufflers have been added to the pipes on their way to the common silencer at the rear.

It felt almost weird when this 962 start­ed from cold at the turn of a key and sat happily there, unattended, burbling away at around 1500 rpm. In the good old days, before electronic engine man­agement, such a car would have needed at least five minutes of throttle blipping to keep the plugs clear and prevent stall­ing. The Porsche noise isn’t music either, not like a Ferrari V-12 or even a Cosworth V-8; it has a rather flat, me­chanical sound. But the tone of the roadgoing 962 is authentic—it’s just muted.

From inside, the exhaust can’t be heard at all. Which is not to say that all is quiet; the harsh clatter of camshafts and valve gear and the whirring of belt drives from just behind your head would make earplugs desirable for a long journey. Schuppan considered fitting some sound insulation but concluded that it would not square with the customer’s desire to drive something as near as possible to a Le Mans racer. He promises that the 962CR will be quieter. It will use a somewhat less highly strung twelve-valve 3.4-liter IMSA motor but with twin turbochargers, developing about 600 horsepower. Entirely air-cooled, it will be less complicated than the Le Mans specification. The 962CR engines are be­ing prepared by Alwin Springers Andial Corporation in the U.S.

The 962CR is also designed to be somewhat easier to live with. We were warned that the Le Mans car is not all that easy to drive. Actually, in terms of operating the controls, the only tricky one is the clutch—a stiff, single-plate, sudden­-engaging competition piece. Race driv­ers know that you can’t feather the pedal: it’s in, or it’s out, and the revs had better be correct as you shift from gear to gear.

The five-speed gearbox has synchromesh—well, Porsche did more or less invent the current variety—and the shifter, at your right hand, works easy enough except when engaging first. That’s a pull down toward you and can require two hands. Second through fifth gear are in a conventional H-pattern.

No one who has driven a racing car would be surprised by the feel of the brake pedal, especially the effort re­quired to operate the massive Alcon cali­pers. In truth, though, it is not unduly heavy. The steering, however, is much lighter than you might expect—at gentle cruising speed it requires little more than finger pressure. Some delicacy is needed here, as there are only one and a half turns from lock to lock, causing an un­schooled driver to overcorrect. The Porsche factory used to fit a slower steer­ing ratio to make the car less nervous on the long Mulsanne straight at Le Mans, and Schuppan thinks that his customers might prefer that.

Drive down a sinuous single-track main road in England, and you can almost imagine pushing through the esses at Le Mans. At anything like normal road speeds, talking about handling isn’t ap­propriate: It just steers precisely where you point it. On smooth asphalt it feels terrific, but on the ridges of a concrete motorway the suspension is so stiff that it sends shock waves through the hull.

The busy roads of southeast England are no place to set track speeds, because the 962 will send every police radar gun off the scale and a bright-red Group C racer has no place to hide. Pulled over in a Porsche 962, you can’t say, “Officer, there must be some mistake.”

Circumstances and discretion did not allow us to discover the merits of this race car’s true performance. We got up to about 120 mph, which felt hardly differ­ent than going 60 mph. A 962 only gets into its stride at 150 mph.

Much less ferocious cars have felt more eager. You know that this one can go on to great and glorious speeds, but you would have to screw yourself up to a racer’s pitch to start from rest strongly enough to beat a serious road-smoker from the traffic lights.

How fast can it go? “How fast do you want to go?” came the reply. Actually, that’s in two parts: It depends on the gearing and turbo boost. For our outing, the shorter of two axle ratios was fitted, so that the maximum speed at the redline would be about 170 mph. With a pukka Le Mans final drive, at least 215 mph should be attainable. We were running with just 14.5-psi boost, but it can be in­creased to 34.8 at the twist of a knob be­hind the gear lever. Schuppan’s engi­neers predict that the fully equipped road car will accelerate from 0 to 60 mph in 3.8 seconds and from 0 to 125 mph in 8.5 seconds.

Schuppan wants to give his customers the opportunity to drive their cars to the track, fit them with stiffer springs, drop the ride height, bolt on some slicks, add some numbers, and go racing. He is even considering a demonstration by entering a 962CR in the Daytona 24 Hours.

In the meantime, he has built up his operation in High Wycombe, some 30 miles west of London, to cope with the transition from racing (Team Schuppan is competing this year in selected Group C events in Europe and Japan) to road­car manufacture, albeit at a rate of one completed car a month. Some 35 per­sons are employed on the project. A key appointment was Ray Borrett as vice president of Vern Schuppan Limited. A fellow Australian, Borrett had been in charge of prototypes and special projects at Holden.

If the first examples of the 962CR are produced on schedule in October, they will have arrived ahead of the Jaguar XJ220, the McLaren F1, the Bugatti T110, and all the other claimants as king of the supercars. Porsche decided some time ago not to proceed with a similar project of its own, and Mercedes now seems unlikely to go ahead with its C112 project. So, for the moment, Schuppan’s Porsches, as sold by Art Sport Division of Japan, may be the fastest truly street­-legal car in the world. They are certainly the most expensive.

Can’t see when backing up? Just switch on the camera.

Howden Ganley, a former Formula 1 driver and Schuppan’s right-hand man, and I came back from our drive in the 962R with smiles on our faces.

Hell, just being in a Le Mans Porsche on ordinary roads is a special kind of fun. Something bright red that makes a fierce, throaty noise, takes up as much room as a truck but sits no higher than a baby buggy, is guaran­teed to get attention, especially in the quiet, respectable villages of Buckinghamshire.

Unwisely perhaps, we set off down a country lane like those you see in old English movies, with chipper chaps in flat hats driving MGs. But when ap­proaching cars saw us come into view, they stopped in their tracks—not, I thought, out of admiration but in real concern that the thing thundering to­ward them promised to take all the available road space, theirs and ours.

Away from the highway—and the racetrack—the 962’s sheer size inhib­its speed. It is wider than a Testarossa, lower than a Diablo, and provides less visibility for the driver than either, though you sit closer to the front than in anything other than a van. It’s right-hand-drive, but there is so much car on either side of the driver that it is effectively a central-seater.

You have to be fit and open-mind­ed to clamber aboard the 962. Stretch your left leg forward onto the seat and around the steering wheel, while sup­porting yourself on the chassis side above the radiators. Then ease the right leg in, holding onto the roof as you slide down. The doors, which fold forward against their hinges, close snugly.

The cockpit is not a place for the claustrophobic. The side windows are a couple of inches from your head, and the shaded top of the windshield is the same distance forward. Driver and passenger can’t help rubbing shoulders. The close-fitting leather-­covered seats are just an inch apart. This is a race car that, aside from FISA regulations, was designed just for a driver.

The thick, padded, and suede-covered steering wheel is perfectly placed, with the little gearshift only a couple of inches away. The column stalks are rather ordinary, and what in a race car is the radio button has been converted to work the horn. The plain matte-black dash panel presents the main instruments ahead—nice clear dials, including a speedometer, spe­cially made by Stack—and is angled on the left-hand side for the digital displays of pressure and tempera­tures. That constricts the passenger’s space even more.

Maneuvering a 962 in a restricted space is a nightmare. It has no rear window, but not to worry: there’s a video camera in the tail—turned on when reverse gear is engaged—but it doesn’t give much of a view (fuzzy at that) on a tiny dashboard screen. Traffic regulations insist that mirrors be placed on the outside edge of the car; they adjust from inside, electrical­ly. By race-car standards, the rear view is panoramic; but on the road, even a lane change needs a lot of care.

We didn’t measure the turning cir­cle, but it is, well, considerable, thanks to a wheelbase like a Detroit land yacht and limited steering lock.

But still, we negotiated normal roads and normal traffic without problem. Neither the car nor we be­came overheated. Fast or slow, all six cylinders fired on cue. Yes, we said, this proves that you can put a Le Mans racer on the road.

Then we smiled again. Neither of us could think of any good reason why anyone would want to. Except for the hell of it. —RH

Leeds fans booing Mathys Tel the latest example of bizarre (but hilarious) fan behaviour

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Leeds United‘s Premier League match at home to Tottenham Hotspur was full of fun little quirks.

There were a few perfect examples of the sort of football fascinations and irritations you might hear discussed on the Football Cliches podcast’s Mesut Haaland Dicks.

But none were more amusing than the reason behind Mathys Tel being booed.

So, why did the Tottenham attacker get booed? Because he was on the receiving end of an absolute horror challenge from Jayden Bogle.

There appeared to be no malice in Bogle’s tackle, if you can even call it that, as he lunged for the ball with one foot and, unfortunately, planted the studs of the other right into Tel’s Achilles.

It looked extremely painful and Tel received treatment for around five minutes, which gave VAR the freedom to review the incident for longer than usual. The conclusion they came to was that it was not worthy of a red card.

To fittingly use a cliché, it was One Of Those. If it’s your team’s player, you’re saying it’s purely accidental and not a red card. If it’s against one of your players, it’s a bloody stonewaller.

And when play finally resumed, every Tel touch was greeted with loud boos from the home support, which made us chuckle and think: is this the most bizarre fan behaviour in football?

Obviously, match-going fans don’t have the benefit of replays, which means they can only go off what they’ve seen live at the time. If they choose to believe Tel is playacting, that’s their prerogative.

Will a player stay down for that long and limp around after returning to action if they aren’t actually hurt? Obviously not.

And when a player subsequently has to go off injured, it’s even better. Definitely milking it, aren’t they?

That’s what happened to Tel, who left the field shortly after play resumed, and you really can’t blame him. It was a terrible challenge from Bogle, who was very, very lucky to avoid a red card.

But the boos from the home support sparked something in us. Getting booed for being absolutely crunched will never stop being funny. It really is brilliant. How dare he!

Honestly, that’s it, really. Football fans are utterly bizarre and it’s class. Their own player can do no wrong in that situation. Bogle was completely innocent, having merely breathed near Tel.

5. Our Favorite: These Levi’s 726 jeans are proof that you don’t need an exaggerated flare to make a statement. Their high-rise waist creates definition up top, while the extra-roomy legs give them that relaxed, oversized silhouette that’s everywhere right now. Style them with a fitted baby tee and retro sneakers for an easy off-duty look.

10. Baggy Done Better: Worried that oversized denim will completely hide your shape? These loose-fitting blue jeans feature a high-rise waist that adds definition, while the wide legs create a relaxed silhouette without clinging to your thighs. Their stretchy fabric also makes them a comfortable choice for long travel days, coffee runs and everything in between.

11. Our Favorite: If you love the sophistication of tailored trousers but prefer the comfort of denim, these Levi’s wide-leg jeans offer the best of both worlds. Their shaping fit creates definition through the hips, while the long, sweeping legs give them an elegant, leg-lengthening finish. Pair them with a fitted cashmere sweater and pointed-toe boots for a polished fall outfit.

'PlayStation Exclusive' Branding Added to Tentpole PS5 Games as Sony Doubles Down

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

For a period of the PS5 generation, Sony ported its games to the PC, rendering very few of its titles genuine exclusives.

In the last year and a bit it’s made an internal decision to focus solely on exclusivity, and it’s slowly beginning to promote that again.

As spotted by power user Radec on social media, the official PlayStation.com pages for Ghost of Yotei and Until Dawn 2 now include ‘PlayStation Exclusive’ logos.

While the manufacturer experimented with bringing its first-party titles to Steam, sales were allegedly poor.

This proves the company is now doubling down on exclusivity again, although it has said it’ll continue to release select multiplayer and live service games on PC, such as this year’s Marvel Tokon: Fighting Souls.

I imagine we’re going to see Sony start to tout the ‘PlayStation Exclusive’ branding more aggressively once it rebuilds its library, beginning with next year’s God of War Laufey and (hopefully) Intergalactic: The Heretic Prophet.

ICYMI: Call Of Duty: Warzone Is Now Available For Switch 2

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Much of the focus has been on Zelda: Ocarina of Time this week, but in case you missed it, Call of Duty: Warzone is now available to download for the Switch 2.

This is a free-to-play battle royale experience, where you can squad up and drop in. It's a separate mode from the Modern Warfare 4 single-player, multiplayer and DMZ mode.

It can be downloaded via the eShop and requires an Activision account to play. On our end, our total file size is now 122GB.

"The start of Season 1 marks the launch of Call of Duty: Warzone on Nintendo Switch™ 2! Drop into Hajin, Zodiac, and all the other maps and playlists as the free-to-play game arrives on the handheld system for the first time."

Alongside this is the release of Season 1 for Modern Warfare 4 and Warzone. In the MW4 multiplayer, this seasonal update comes loaded with additional multiplayer maps (Backstab, Canyon, Shoot House and more), modes, events, and rewards across both the game's multiplayer and DMZ. Skip Ground Chuck: The Ideal Type Of Ground Beef For Tacos With Big Flavor

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

It goes without saying that meat forms the cornerstone of a ground beef taco. Yet while so many cooks focus on adding something (like cocoa powder) to ground beef for richer tacos, fewer pay attention to the cut at hand. After all, minced meat takes on not only various fat compositions, but can originate from different parts of the animal, each of which translates into a unique flavor and texture.

Especially common on grocery shelves is a product simply labeled ground beef, which means the meat mix consists of bits and pieces sourced from all around the cow. Also popular is ground chuck, a cut that turns out rich when ground, perfect for burgers and meatballs that will hold their shape during cooking. For tacos, though, reach for ground round instead; it's the ideal type for a flavorful pan-fried filling.

Round comes from a broad region near the back legs of a cow. It's an area high in connective tissue, but leaner than chuck. As a result, the ground beef doesn't turn too greasy when cooked, instead producing lean bites of meat that are amenable to the dish. The flavor is milder, too — making it an ideal canvas for your chosen seasonings. Put together, such characteristics yield a tasty beef character, but one that won't overwhelm your taco build.

Making the best ground beef taco meat is different from other recipes. Fat is flavor, and with the round's leaner composition than chuck, you're getting a less beefy experience. If you're looking to make juicy burger patties, that's a detractor. For ground beef tacos, though, such a restrained character is beneficial.

Aim for a ground round composition of around 85% lean and 15% fat beef. There's still enough fat to keep bites tender, but not an excess that'll have oil pooling in the pan. You'll let the intricate flavor of taco seasonings shine, but the beef still comes with its own delicate nuance. You can get unorthodox and add some of the best seasonings for beef, like pairing Guajillo chile powder with honey.

Furthermore, the ground round bits will cook down into a crumble, which lets you adhere spice onto a greater surface area. And once rolled into a tortilla or crispy taco shell, the beef is loose in tiny pieces, as is classic with the dish. Ground round strikes a perfect balance, making a taco rendition that hits the right notes without overwhelming the dish.

How to Winterize Salvias – 5 Easy Ways to Protect Plants and Guarantee Dazzling Blooms Next Year

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

7 Foolproof Plants Anyone Can Propagate From Cuttings in November – Grow More of Your Favorite Shrubs for Free

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Many salvias can tough it out through fall, but even these easygoing plants need a helping hand in winter. Make time for our smart overwintering tips to protect these iconic favorites

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As a seasoned gardener, if you ask me to name an easy-to-grow flowering perennial that returns reliably year after year, salvia would be at the top of my list. In my own garden, my ‘Hot Lips’ shrubby salvia shrugs off summer drought, asks for very little maintenance, and blooms generously from late spring through to November. Their nectar-rich flower spikes are magnets for hummingbirds, bees, and butterflies, drenching borders in rich purples, electric blues, hot pinks, and velvety crimsons.

Yet, despite their tough and easygoing nature, winter is the one season where salvias do need a bit of care and attention. The genus Salvia is vast, encompassing over a thousand species of annuals, woody sub-shrubs, and herbaceous perennials from regions as diverse as cold alpine meadows, dry Mediterranean hillsides, and tropical cloud forests. Because their natural habitats differ dramatically, their winter cold tolerance does, too. What protects a hardy meadow sage in New England will not save a tender Mexican bush salvia during a sudden polar snap. So winter salvia care needs to be adapted accordingly.

Taking action now, in this narrow window between autumn's close and winter's first freeze, is critical. Winter damage can happen fast, and yet knowing how to winterize salvias is surprisingly easy. By understanding your variety's cold tolerance and taking action fast, you can help prime your salvia in winter. Here’s how to easily guide your garden favorites through to next spring, so you can enjoy their lush, vibrant blooms for years to come.

The precise winter protection required will depend on where you live and the type of salvia you are growing. Some are cold-hardy perennials, while others are tender, frost-sensitive tropicals. So successful winterizing begins with identifying growth habit and hardiness classification. Deciduous herbaceous salvias like meadow sage (S. nemorosa cultivars like Caradonna and May Night) and woodland sage (S. pratensis) are cold-hardy in USDA hardiness zones 3-8. These perennials die back after hard frosts, resting safely underground before surging back in spring. They need little winter protection beyond good drainage and light insulation.

Shrubby, semi-hardy salvias (S. greggii and S. microphylla) occupy a middle ground, thriving in zones 6-9. These semi-evergreen sub-shrubs develop woody stems that can be damaged by sudden cold snaps or damp winter soils. In colder microclimates within these zones, they require protective mulching and shelter from wet winds. Finally, tender subtropical and tropical salvias, including velvet-flowered Amistad and Mexican bush sage (S. leucantha), flourish only in frost-free zones 8-11. These are large, bushy salvias that are killed by hard winter freezes, so in cooler zones, they require extra winter protection.

Before you begin, test your soil moisture levels and check that water drains away readily after rain. The XLUX Long Probe Deep Soil Moisture Meter, available from Amazon is a great dual soil moisture and pH diagnostic probe, giving instant readings to help you identify waterlogged soil pockets before the ground freezes. Once your soil is identified, you’re ready to take action. Here we clarify when to cut your salvias back, how to protect the roots from cold and wet conditions, and how to ensure you see your jewel-toned flowering favorites again next year.

When salvias start looking ragged, the natural impulse is to reach for the bypass pruners and shear everything back to a tidy clump. This could be the most damaging mistake you make. Many perennial salvias (especially semi-woody and herbaceous varieties) have hollow, straw-like stems. Pruning these down in late fall leaves those open tubes exposed to the elements. Rain and melting snow funnel down the stems and collect at the base, causing crown rot and fungal decay in freeze-thaw cycles.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

It’s best to leave these old stems, faded flower spikes, and withered foliage in place to keep driving rain away from the vulnerable center. This dried top growth also traps air, forming an insulating buffer that protects the dormant buds huddled around the crown. So resist all heavy pruning until early spring, when fresh shoots begin pushing through the soil. Even if you live in a warm-winter area, it’s still best to leave major pruning until spring. The only fall cuts you should make are removing broken or diseased stems. Use sharp bypass pruners like the Felco 2 Bypass Pruning Shears, available from Amazon, when removing broken salvia stems.

Whenever I mulch plants for winter, I think of tucking a blanket around a sleeping child. Mulching salvias as cold weather is coming offers a layer of winter protection. When you apply mulch before the first hard freeze, you protect the root crown from severe cold and freeze-heave. In regions with fluctuating winter weather, bare soil expands and contracts, pushing shallow crowns up and exposing roots. Fully hardy salvia don’t need much winter protection, but a light mulch won’t hurt. When it comes to the semi-hardy salvias, however, mulching is a must to help overwintering plants.

The type of mulch, and how you apply it, are critical to avoid trapping moisture around the plant. Avoid dense, heavy materials like wet grass clippings, uncomposted manure, or thick leaf mold, which pack down and suffocate the plant. Instead, apply 3-4 inches (8-10cm) of loose, breathable mulch such as shredded pine bark, clean wheat straw, dry bracken, or horticultural grit. Keep it 2-3 inches (5-8cm) from the stems to avoid trapping moisture and inviting crown rot. Buy Bell Nursery Mini Straw Bales from The Home Depot for a loose yet insulating blanket.

Cold isn’t necessarily fatal for salvia plants in winter. But too much water will take out your salvias faster than a freeze. Native to sun-drenched Mediterranean climes, salvias demand sharp drainage. When sluggish drainage keeps soil saturated, oxygen is pushed out of the roots, leaving the crown vulnerable to fungal diseases. You’ll want to take steps to avoid poorly drained soil and winter wetness for any type of salvia.

If your garden has heavy clay soil, take action now before winter sets in. Gently scrape away decomposing leaves, as wet leaf mats retain moisture over the crown. Work a handful of coarse horticultural grit, sand or perlite into the soil surface around the plant to speed up surface runoff. Back to the Roots Organic Perlite from Walmart is a great option for improving soil aeration and drainage around salvias. For container-grown plants, elevate the pots off cold, wet patio slabs. Use terracotta pot feet, pot toes, or bricks to ensure drainage holes remain clear of standing water.

If your salvia are tender varieties like Amistad or S. leucantha, treat them like the cold vulnerable plants that they are. Sub-tropical salvias can’t tolerate hard frosts or frozen soil. Leaving them unprotected in northern yards guarantees root death. Fortunately, tender salvias overwinter indoors with minimal fuss, provided you bring them into shelter before the first hard frost blackens foliage. If your tender salvias are in pots, trim the longest branches back by half, then move to an unheated garage, frost-free shed, cold frame, or cool porch, and keep at 35-45°F (2-7°C).

For tender salvias in beds, carefully dig up the root ball with a spade, keeping plenty of soil around the roots. Place into a plastic nursery container, backfill loosely with dry potting mix, and move it undercover. Use plant caddies such as the Bloem Plant Caddy from The Home Depot to wheel larger container-based salvias into the garage with zero strain or back pain. The goal is to keep the plant dormant. Water sparingly: just a cup or two a month keeps the root ball from drying out.

Even with meticulous winter care, an unseasonal polar vortex or prolonged freeze can challenge borderline-hardy salvias. Taking a few semi-ripe salvia cuttings provides a terrific insurance policy. Select healthy, non-flowering or semi-ripe shoots (or snip the flower tip off a vigorous stem). Take a 3-4-inch (8-10cm) cutting below a leaf node. Strip the leaves from the bottom half, leaving 2-3 pairs of small leaves at the top. Dip into a rooting hormone such as HydroDynamics Clonex Rooting Gel from Amazon, to seal cut stems and trigger root growth. Insert the cuttings into small pots filled with a 50/50 mix of sterile seed compost and perlite.

Water gently, then place a clear plastic dome or bag over the pot to maintain humidity. Set your baby cuttings on a bright, warm windowsill, but out of direct sunlight. They should root within 3-4 weeks. Keep them lightly watered through winter, and pinch the tips in late winter to encourage bushy growth. Just bear in mind that rooting will be slower now than it would be with cuttings taken in late summer or early fall. Grown on a sunny indoor windowsill or greenhouse bench through winter, your salvia babies will hopefully be ready to plant out next May.

By the time spring arrives, you’ll be eager for warmer days and fresh growth (and so will your salvias!). As sunny afternoons begin warming the soil, check your overwintered salvias for vibrant green shoots emerging. That is your signal that winter danger has passed. You can then prune away old standing stems just above the fresh growth nodes. If you have woody salvias, spring is the time to shape the plant. Cut it back by a third to clean up the shrub. Spring is also the time to pull back protective mulches and reintroduce sheltered container plants to outdoor sunshine. With their crowns preserved through the cold months, your salvias will soon be rewarding you with months of lush and lively color.

Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. julia lohmann’s otherworldly kelp sculptures imagine a future beyond human-centered design

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

from leather-covered cow benches to towering kelp sculptures, julia lohmann questions how humans turn living organisms into design materials.

At London’s Design Museum, German-born designer Julia Lohmann arranged five towering kelp sculptures in a circle for the 2025 exhibition More than Human. Their translucent skins stretch across ribbed frameworks, resembling unfamiliar creatures brought up from the ocean floor. Titled Kelp Council, the installation imagines a gathering of marine organisms discussing the condition of their environment, with visitors invited to join the conversation.

As designers increasingly turn to seaweed as an alternative to conventional materials, Lohmann raises a question about the ecosystems supplying them. What happens when a natural resource becomes the focus of growing industrial demand? Through her sculptures and material experiments, she considers how design might work with nature while accounting for the living systems behind its materials.

Julia Lohmann’s interest in the origins of everyday materials began long before her experiments with seaweed. In 2004, she created Waltraud Cow-Bench, a leather-covered piece of furniture shaped to resemble a reclining cow. By returning the familiar material to the form of the animal it came from, she draws attention to a relationship that typically disappears during production.

This thinking eventually led her toward marine algae. During a 2013 residency at London’s Victoria and Albert Museum, she founded the Department of Seaweed, a collaborative research initiative exploring the properties of kelp and its potential applications in design. Working with scientists and other specialists, the designer began developing techniques that would allow the material’s natural characteristics to shape her sculptural forms.

In Kombu Ahtola (2020), pieces of seaweed are stretched between the ribs of a rattan framework. As the kelp dries, it contracts, pulling the structure into its curved shape. The material helps determine the sculpture’s final geometry, introducing a degree of unpredictability into the making process.

Her Hidaka Ohmu pavilion, also presented in 2020, brings these experiments to an architectural scale. Measuring approximately six meters across, the structure is covered in translucent seaweed panels that filter light into its curved interior. Visitors can move through the installation, experiencing the unusual texture and physical presence of a material more commonly associated with the ocean.

Kelp forests support complex marine ecosystems, providing habitats for numerous species. While seaweed offers promising possibilities for material development, expanding its cultivation and harvesting introduces ecological questions of its own. Lohmann’s research considers how these resources might be used while preserving the environments that sustain them.

Kelp Council brings this concern into a more speculative setting. By imagining marine organisms gathered around a discussion, Lohmann asks whose interests enter the decisions we make about natural resources. Her sculptures offer a physical expression of an idea that connects her early work with her ongoing seaweed research: that the future of sustainable design depends as much on how we obtain materials as on what we make from them.

JuliaLohmann, Oki Naganode, V&A London, 2013 | image courtesy the designerJulia Lohmann, Oki Naganode, MoMA, 2013 | image courtesy MoMA

My 9 Favorite Hotel Toiletry Brands, From Aesop To Le Labo, And More

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Luxury hotel brands try to differentiate themselves in all kinds of ways. One (arguably) minor way they do so is by partnering with other luxury brands on their toiletry and skincare lines. While I’m not suggesting that you should pick a hotel brand because of the toiletries that they offer, I think I’m not alone in preferring some brands to others.

In this post, I’d like to share my favorite hotel toiletry brands, and then I’m curious to hear what brands OMAAT readers prefer. To be clear, my rankings are based purely on my perception of products and brands, from the packaging, to the scent, to how it feels using them, to memories I associate with them. I’m not claiming any authority with my opinion, so please don’t ask me to defend my preferences!

Still, I’m sure I’m not alone in getting more excited about some toiletry brands than others. With that out of the way, below are some of my favorite hotel toiletry brands (let me also emphasize that not every single property belonging to these groups has the brand standard toiletries).

Le Labo generally supplies toiletries for EDITION and Park Hyatt hotels, as well as select Fairmont properties, and it’s probably my single favorite toiletry brand. I love how there are different scents, and how they’re often customized for individual hotels (with the branding on the bottles even reflecting that). I enjoy the scents, and also appreciate that they seem to be fairly mild.

Bamford toiletries are generally available at ultra-luxury hotel group Capella, and sister brand Patina. I absolutely love Bamford, and find the product scents to be pleasant, and I also like the simplicity of the branding. For airline geeks, Bamford is also nowadays the premium toiletry partner of Cathay Pacific, so don’t be surprised if you see these products in Cathay Pacific lounges, and onboard the carrier’s flights.

Within the past few years, Ritz-Carlton has started partnering with Diptyque on toiletries. What an absolutely massive upgrade this is, compared to the previous Asprey Purple Rain collection, which I personally found to be completely unremarkable. I find Diptyque to have a very pleasant scent without being overpowering. It’s something I otherwise associate with Qatar Airways, as it’s the only major airline to partner with the brand. Speaking of changes in recent years in the luxury Marriott portfolio, St. Regis has moved to SACHAJUAN, which I find to be unmemorable.

A few years ago, Waldorf Astoria made the switch from Salvatore Ferragamo toiletries to Aesop toiletries. I’d consider that to be a significant upgrade. I love the scent of Aesop, and it can’t help but always remind me of Cathay Pacific back in the day (which, as noted above, has now switched to Bamford).

I love BVLGARI toiletries, including the packaging, scent, and feeling of using them. The catch is that while many hotels have BVLGARI toiletries, there’s not much consistency as to which brands have them. Yes, as you’d expect, BVLGARI hotels have these products, but that’s a small hotel group, with under 10 properties. On top of that, you’ll find BVLGARI toiletries at select properties of Four Seasons, Rosewood, and more.

Acqua Di Parma is another one of my favorite hotel toiletry brands, in terms of packaging and scent. The catch is that there’s not a major hotel group that consistently stocks these products, though they’re common enough among hotel groups that I feel good about including it on the list. For example, while not brand standard, the St. Regis Venice uses Acqua Di Parma products.

Molton Brown makes great products (I particularly like the soap). While no major hotel group consistently has Molton Brown products, I find that they’re pretty common at luxury hotels, including at many Marriott Luxury Collection properties.

I think Byredo is worth mentioning, as you’ll find the Bal d’Afrique product line at many InterContinental properties, and the Le Chemin product line at many Luxury Collection properties. While I don’t like this quite as much as some of the brands mentioned above, I appreciate that it’s often available at more mid-range properties, where you won’t typically find brands like Aesop and Le Labo.

Admittedly this is a bit lower end than the other options, but I wanted to also throw in a toiletry brand from a more mid-range hotel brand. For upscale hotels, I’d say that MALIN+GOETZ, available at Le Meridien properties, is probably my favorite line.

The above are nine of my favorite hotel toiletry lines. As you can see, some are pretty consistently available at select hotel brands, while others are frequently offered, though not consistently at any brand. I don’t claim that my opinions are right, but the above are the toiletry brands I’m typically most excited to see at hotels.

What are your favorite hotel toiletry brands?
